is limit. Furthermore, the set of all
X\in\mathscr{P}(\gamma)
for which
\mathcal{F}|_{X}
is a construction scheme can be fully described as

\mathcal{F}\cup(\text{Lim}\cap(\gamma+1)).

In the case where
\gamma
is infinite, the previous family has
\omega
as a special member. This is because, according to the Theorem
5.0.17
,
\mathcal{F}|_{\omega}
is simply
\mathcal{F}(\omega)
. This scheme in
\omega
stands out not only because of its uniqueness but because each
m_{k}
belongs to it. Therefore,
m_{k}
not only acts as the common cardinality of all elements in
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, but it is the “most canonical” member of such family. Actually, this line of thought transfers to all natural numbers. Concretely, since the initial segments of closed sets are closed according to Corollary
2.1.8
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.0.21
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a construction scheme over an infinite ordinal
\gamma
. Then
n\in\omega
is a closed set with respect to
\rho
. Furthermore,
n\in(m_{\rho^{n}-1},m_{\rho^{n}}]
whenever
n>0
.

The metric structure of closed sets is fully determined by their cardinality (see Lemma
2.1.27
and Theorem
2.1.26
). In particular, for each
\beta\in\gamma
and
l\in\omega
, the set
(\beta)_{l}
is
\rho
-isomorphic to
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}+1=[0,\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}]
(recall
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}=|(\beta)^{-}_{l}|
). It will be convenient to name and explicitly describe the increasing bijections between these two sets.

Definition 5.0.22
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a construction scheme over a limit ordinal
\gamma
. Given
\beta\in\gamma
and
l\in\omega
, we define
\phi^{\beta}_{l}:(\beta)_{l}\longrightarrow\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}+1
as:

\phi^{\beta}_{l}(\alpha)=\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l}.

The inverse function of
\phi^{\beta}_{l}
is given by:

(\phi^{\beta}_{l})^{-1}(i)=(\beta)_{l}(i).

Proposition 5.0.23
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a construction scheme over an ordinal
\gamma
. Given
\alpha,\beta\in\gamma
and
k\leqslant l\in\omega
, the following conditions hold:

(a)

\lVert\beta\rVert_{k}=\lVert\,\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\,\rVert_{k},

(b)

If
l\geqslant\rho(\alpha,\beta)
, then
\rho(\alpha,\beta)=\rho(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l},\lVert\beta\rVert_{l})

(c)

If
l\geqslant\Delta(\alpha,\beta)
, then
\Delta(\alpha,\beta)=\Delta(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l},\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}).

We can rewrite the point (a) of the previous proposition in a more useful way.

Lemma 5.0.24
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a construction scheme over an ordinal
\gamma
. Given
\beta\in\gamma
and
k\leqslant l\in\omega
, we have that

\phi^{\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}}_{k}\circ\phi^{\beta}_{l}=\phi^{\beta}_{k}.

5.1
The forcing
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
and schemes in ZFC

In this section we will present an incarnation of the Cohen forcing which was first considered in
[
112
]
. This forcing will serve the purpose of extending a construction scheme from a countable limit ordinal to the next one, and will play a key roll in the arguments of the next sections of this chapter. As an application, we will show in subsection
5.1.1
that there is a construction scheme over
\omega_{1}
of any given type. All of this section is based in
[
112
]
.

For the rest of this section, let us fix a construction scheme
\mathcal{F}
of type
\tau
over a limit ordinal
\gamma
.

Definition 5.1.1

(The forcing
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
)
.

We define
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
as the forcing consisting of the empty set and of all
p\in\text{FIN}(\gamma+\omega)
with the following properties:

(I)

There is
k_{p}\in\omega
such that
|p|=m_{k_{p}}
.

(II)

There is
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k_{p}}
such that
p\cap\gamma\sqsubseteq F
.

(III)

p\cap[\gamma,\gamma+\omega)
is an initial segment of
[\gamma,\gamma+\omega)
.

Whenever
p\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset
(even if
p
is not a condition of
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
), we let
\alpha_{p}=\max(p\cap\gamma)
. Additionally, for each
k\in\omega
we let
\mathbb{P}_{k}(\mathcal{F})=\{\,p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\,:\,k_{p}=k\,\}.
The order on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
is given by

p\leqslant q\text{ if and only if }q\in\mathcal{F}(p)\textit{ or }q=\emptyset.

Note that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
is always countable. Therefore, it is forcing equivalent to the Cohen forcing.

In here,
p
represents an element of
\mathbb{P}_{k}(\mathcal{F})
and
F
represents an element of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
which testifies the condition (II) of Definition
5.1.1
for
p
.

Remark 5.1.2
.

From Lemma
2.2.13
it follows that the condition (II) of Definition
5.1.1
is equivalent to:

(II)
{}^{*}\>\>p\cap\gamma=(\alpha_{p})_{k_{p}}
,

in the case where
p\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset.

In general, if
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
, there are many elements
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k_{p}}
testifying the condition (II) of Definition
5.1.1
with respect to
p
. However, it turns out that there is a canonical one. Such
F
will be useful in many of the arguments involving the forcing
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. In the following definition, we explicitly describe it.

Definition 5.1.3

(The reduction operation)
.

If
p\in\text{FIN}(\gamma+\omega)
and
\delta\leqslant\gamma
, we define the
reduction of
p
to
\delta
as follows:

red_{\delta}(p)=\begin{cases}(p\cap\delta)\cup[\max(p\cap\delta)+1,\max(p\cap\delta)+|p\backslash\delta|+1)&\textit{ if }p\cap\delta\not=\emptyset\\
|p|&\textit{ if }p\cap\delta=\emptyset\end{cases}

Remark 5.1.4
.

The reduction operation is closely related to the Corollary
5.0.15
. Suppose that we take
\alpha\in\gamma
and
k\in\omega
. According to the corollary, we know that there is a unique
E\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
so that
\alpha\in E
and
E\backslash\alpha
is an interval in
\gamma
. It turns out that such
E
can be described using the reduction operation. In order to do this, take an arbitrary
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
with
\alpha\in F
. Then:

\displaystyle red_{\alpha+1}(F)

\displaystyle=F\cap(\alpha+1)\cup[\,\alpha+1,\,\alpha+1+|F\backslash(\alpha+1)|\,)

\displaystyle=(\alpha)^{-}_{k}\cup[\,\alpha,\,\alpha+1+(m_{k}-(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k}+1))\,)

\displaystyle=(\alpha)^{-}_{k}\cup[\,\alpha,\,\alpha+m_{k}-\lVert\alpha\rVert\,).

Due to the Remark
5.0.16
, it is straightforward that
E=red_{\alpha+1}(F)
.

The reduction operation will be mainly used in the case where
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
and
\alpha=\gamma
. We now illustrate such situation for convenience of the reader.

By arguing in a similar manner as in the Remark
5.1.4
, we get the following lemma. The proof of it is left to the reader.

Lemma 5.1.5
.

If
p\in\text{FIN}(\gamma+\omega)
is such that
p\cap[\gamma,\gamma+\omega)
is an initial segment of
[\gamma,\gamma+\omega)
, then the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
,

(b)

red_{\gamma}(p)\in\mathcal{F}.

Even though the previous lemma is useful, the main tool for defining and extending conditions in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
relies in the next definition.

Definition 5.1.6

(The cut operation)
.

Let
F\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
and
\alpha\in\gamma
. We define the
cut of
F
at
\alpha
as follows:

Cut_{\alpha}(F)=(F\cap\alpha)\cup[\gamma,\gamma+|F\backslash\alpha|)

The following picture illustrates the previous definition for the particular case in which
\alpha\in F
. This is the main situation in which the cut operation will be applied.

Remark 5.1.7
.

Suppose that
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
\alpha\in\gamma
. If
\phi:F\longrightarrow Cut_{\alpha}(F)
is the increasing bijection, then

\mathcal{F}(Cut_{\alpha}(F))=\{\,\phi[H]\,:\,H\in\mathcal{F}(F)\,\}

due to the Remark
5.0.10
. In particular, this means that
Cut_{\alpha}(F_{i})
is the
ith
element of the canonical decomposition of
Cut_{\alpha}(F)
for each
i<n_{\rho^{F}}
. In other words,
Cut_{\alpha}(F_{i})=(Cut_{\alpha}(F))_{i}.
On the other hand, it is not necessarily true that
\phi[H]=Cut_{\alpha}(H)
for a given
H\in\mathcal{F}(F)
. A particular case in which the previous equality holds is when
\alpha\in H
and
H\backslash\alpha
is an interval in
F
(Thus, an initial segment of
F\backslash\alpha
).

Remark 5.1.8
.

The reduction and cut operations are in some sense inverses of each other. That is, if
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
and
p\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset
, then

Cut_{\alpha_{p}+1}(red_{\gamma}(p))=p
. On the other hand, if
F\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
and
\alpha\in F
then
red_{\gamma}(Cut_{\alpha+1}(F))=F
.

Lemma 5.1.9
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
\alpha\in\gamma
. Then
Cut_{\alpha}(F)\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. Furthermore, if
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
are such that
F\subseteq G
and
\alpha\in F
then
Cut_{\alpha}(G)\leqslant Cut_{\alpha}(F)
.

Proof.

For the first part of the lemma, suppose that
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
\alpha\in\gamma
. Let
k=\rho^{F}
. Then
m_{k}=|F|=|Cut_{\alpha}(F)|
. In this way,
Cut_{\alpha}(F)
satisfies the condition (I) of Definition
5.1.1
. Now,
Cut_{\alpha}(F)\cap\gamma=F\cap\alpha\sqsubseteq F
. Therefore, the condition (II) of the same definition holds for
Cut_{\alpha}(F)
. Finally,
Cut_{\alpha}(F)\cap[\,\gamma,\,\gamma+\omega\,)=[\,\gamma,\,\gamma+|F\backslash\alpha|\,)
. Thus,
Cut_{\alpha}(F)
satisfies the condition (III) of Definition
5.1.1
, which means that
Cut_{\alpha}(F)\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Next, we prove the second part of the lemma. Let
F,G\in\mathcal{F}
be such that
F\subseteq G
and let
\alpha\in F
. If
F=G
, the result is obvious. So we may assume that the inclusion between
F
and
G
is proper. It follows that
k<l
where
k=\rho^{F}
and
l=\rho^{G}
. Therefore, we are in the conditions of applying the lemma
5.0.14
to
\alpha,G
and
k
. In this way, we get
E\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
for which
\alpha\in E
,
E\subseteq G
and
E\backslash\alpha
is an interval in
G
. Note that
|E\backslash\alpha|=|F\backslash\alpha|
and
F\cap\alpha=(\alpha)^{-}_{k}=E\cap\alpha
. Thus,
Cut_{\alpha}(F)=Cut_{\alpha}(E)
. Now, let
\phi:G\longrightarrow Cut_{\alpha}(G)
be the increasing bijection. Due to the Remark
5.1.7
,

\mathcal{F}(Cut_{\alpha}(G))=\{\,\phi[H]\,:\,H\in\mathcal{F}(G)\,\}.

Finally, just note that
\phi[E]=Cut_{\alpha}(E)
by virtue of the same remark.
∎

Lemma 5.1.10
.

Let
k\in\omega
,
p\in\mathbb{P}_{k}(\mathcal{F})
(that is,
k=k_{p}
) and
\alpha\in\gamma
be such that
(\alpha)^{-}_{k}=p\cap\gamma
. Then
Cut_{\alpha}(G)\leqslant p
for each
G\in\mathcal{F}
with
\alpha\in G
and
\rho^{G}\geqslant k.

Proof.

Let
G\in\mathcal{F}
be as in the hypotheses. Consider
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
for which
\alpha\in F
and
F\subseteq G.
According to the Lemma
5.1.9
,
Cut_{\alpha}(G)\leqslant Cut_{\alpha}(F)
. To finish, just note that
|Cut_{\alpha}(F)|=|p|
and
Cut_{\alpha}(G)\cap\gamma=G\cap\alpha=(\alpha)^{-}_{k}=p\cap\gamma.
In this way,
Cut_{\alpha}(F)=p
due to the condition (III) of Definition
5.1.1
.

∎

5.1.1
Construction schemes in ZFC

The next definition appeared for the first time in
[
112
]
. This hypothesis is sufficient to build construction schemes in a recursive manner.

Definition 5.1.11

(The
IH_{1}
property)
.

Let
A\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
,
\alpha\in\gamma
and
F\in\mathcal{F}
. We say that
IH_{1}(\alpha,A,F)
holds if:

(1)

A\subseteq F_{0},

(2)

R(F)=F\cap\alpha
.

Additionally, we say that
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
if for all
A\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
and
\alpha\in\gamma
, there is
F\in\mathcal{F}
for which
IH_{1}(\alpha,A,F)
holds.

Proposition 5.1.12
.

Suppose that
\tau
is a good type. Then
\mathcal{F}(\omega)
satisfies
IH_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
A\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
and
\alpha\in\omega
. Since
\tau
is a good type, there is
k>\max(A)
for which
r_{k+1}=\alpha
. Let
F=m_{k+1}
. Then
F\in\mathcal{F}(\omega)
,
F_{0}=m_{k}\supseteq A
and
R(F)=r_{k+1}=\alpha
. This finishes the proof.
∎

The following lemma is easy.

Lemma 5.1.13
.

Suppose that
\gamma
is a limit of limit ordinals and
\mathcal{F}|_{\delta}
satisfies
IH_{1}
for each limit
\delta<\gamma
. Then
\mathcal{F}
also satisfies
IH_{1}
.

Definition 5.1.14
.

Given a filter
\mathcal{G}
over
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
, we define
\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{G}}
as
\bigcup_{p\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{F}(p).
Finally,
\mathcal{F}^{Gen}
denotes the name for
\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{G}}
where
G
is a generic filter.

Lemma 5.1.15
.

Suppose that
\tau
is a good type and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
. Then the set
\{\,p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\,:\,p\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset\,\}
is
open dense in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. Without loss of generality we can assume that
q\not=\emptyset
. We need to find
p\leqslant q
such that
p\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset
. If
q\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset
, we are done. So suppose that
q\cap\gamma=\emptyset.
Then
q=[\gamma,\gamma+m_{k_{q}})
due to the condition (III) of Definition
5.1.1
. Since
\tau
is a good type, then there is
k>k_{q}
such that
r_{k+1}=0
. Let
F=m_{k+1}
and
\alpha=m_{k}
. Then
F\in\mathcal{F}(\omega)\subseteq\mathcal{F}
. By means of the
5.1.9
,

m_{k}\cup[\gamma,\gamma+m_{k}+1-m_{k})=Cut_{\alpha}(F)\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Let
p=Cut_{\alpha}(F)
. Note that the second piece of the canonical decomposition of
p
, that is
p_{1}
, is equal to
[\gamma,\gamma+m_{k})
. From this, it is easy to see that
[\gamma,\gamma+m_{j})\in\mathcal{F}(p_{1})
for each
j\leqslant k
. In particular,
q\in\mathcal{F}(p_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{F}(p)
. In other words,
p\leqslant q
. Since
p\cap\gamma=m_{k}\not=\emptyset
, the proof is over.
∎

Lemma 5.1.16
.

Suppose that
\tau
is a good type and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
. Then the set
\{\,p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\,:\,p\cap[\gamma,\gamma+\omega)\not=\emptyset\,\}
is open dense in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
q\not=\emptyset
. We need to find
p\leqslant q
with
p\backslash\gamma\not=\emptyset
. If
q\backslash\gamma\not=\emptyset
, there is nothing to do. In this way, we may assume that
q\subseteq\gamma
. In this case, it follows that
q\in\mathcal{F}
. Let
\alpha=\alpha_{p}+1=\max(q)+1.
Since
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
, there is
F\in\mathcal{F}
with
q\cup\{\alpha\}\subseteq F_{0}
and
\alpha\cap F=R(F)
. Note that
q
is actually a subset of
R(F)
. Let
p=Cut_{\alpha}(F)
. According to the Lemma
5.1.9
,
p=Cut_{\alpha}(F)\leqslant Cut_{\alpha}(q)=q
. Furthermore,
p\backslash\gamma\not=\emptyset
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.1.17
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a construction scheme over
\gamma
satisfying
IH_{1}
. For any
\alpha\in\gamma+\omega
, the set

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\alpha}=\{p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\,:\,\alpha\in p\}

is open dense in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. By Lemmas
5.1.15
and
5.1.16
, we may assume that
q\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset
and
q\backslash\gamma\not=\emptyset
. In this way,
\alpha_{q}
is well defined and
\alpha_{q}+1\in F
where
F=red_{\gamma}(q)
. In particular,
(\alpha_{q}+1)^{-}_{k_{q}}=q\cap\gamma
. Recall that our goal is to find
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
with
p\leqslant q
and such that
\alpha\in p
. The proof of this fact is divided into two cases.

Case 1
: If
\alpha<\gamma
.

Proof of case.

Since
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
, there is
G\in\mathcal{F}
so that
\{\alpha\}\cup F\subseteq G_{0}
and
(\alpha_{q}+1)\cap G=R(G)
. Let
k=\rho^{G}
and
\beta=\min(G_{1}\backslash R(G))
. Note that
k>k_{q}
because
F\subseteq G_{0}
. Moreover,
(\beta)^{-}_{k-1}=R(F)=(\alpha_{q}+1)^{-}_{k-1}
. Since
k_{q}\leqslant k
, it follows that
(\beta)^{-}_{k_{q}}=(\alpha_{q}+1)^{-}_{k_{q}}=q\cap\gamma
. Let
p=Cut_{\beta}(G)
. According to the Lemma
5.1.10
,
p\leqslant q
. Moreover,
p\cap\gamma=G_{0}
and this set contains
\alpha
. Hence, the proof of this case is done.
∎

Case 2
: If
\alpha\geqslant\gamma
.

Proof of case.

Let
n=|\alpha\backslash\gamma|+1
. Again, using that
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
, we can get
G\in\mathcal{F}
so that
F\cup[\alpha_{q},\alpha_{q}+n]\subseteq G_{0}
and
(\alpha_{q}+1)\cap G=R(G)
. By arguing in a similar way as we did in the first case, we may conclude that for
\beta=\min(G_{1}\backslash R(G))
,
p=Cut_{\beta}(G)
is a condition in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
which is smaller than
q
. In order to finish, note that
[\alpha_{q},\alpha_{q}+n]\subseteq G_{0}\backslash R(G)
. Therefore,
n\leqslant|G_{0}\backslash R(G)|=|G_{1}\backslash R(G)|
. According to the definition of
Cut_{\beta}(G)
, we get that
|p\cap[\gamma,\gamma+\omega)|\geqslant n
. Thus,
\alpha\in[\gamma,\gamma+n]\subseteq p
.
∎

∎

Lemma 5.1.18
.

Suppose that
\tau
is a good type and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
. For any
F\in\mathcal{F}
, the set

\mathcal{D}_{F}=\{\,p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\,:\,F\in\mathcal{F}(p)\,\}

is open dense in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathcal{F}
. By applying Lemma
5.1.17
to each element of
F
, we get that there is
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
so that
p\leqslant q
and
F\subseteq p.
According to the Lemma
5.1.9
,
Cut_{\alpha_{p}+1}(red_{\gamma}(p))\leqslant Cut_{\alpha_{p}+1}(F)
. That is,
Cut_{\alpha_{p}+1}(F)\in\mathcal{F}(Cut_{\alpha_{p}+1}(red_{\gamma}(p)))
. Note that
Cut_{\alpha_{p}+1}(F)=F
because
F\subseteq\alpha_{p}+1
. Moreover,
Cut_{\alpha_{p}+1}(red_{\gamma}(p))=p
by virtue of the Remark
5.1.8
. This finishes the proof.

∎

Lemma 5.1.19
.

Suppose that
\tau
is a good type and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
. For any
A\in\text{FIN}(\gamma+\omega)
and each
\alpha\in\gamma+\omega
, the set

\mathcal{E}_{\alpha,A}=\{\,p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\,:\,\alpha\in p,\,A\subseteq p_{0},\textit{ and }\alpha\cap p=R(p)\,\}

is dense in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

Let
q\in\mathbb{P}
. By means of the previous lemmas, we can assume without loss of generality that
q\cap\gamma\not=\emptyset
,
q\backslash\gamma\not=\emptyset
and
\{\alpha\}\cup A\subseteq q
. Let
\alpha^{\prime}=red_{\gamma}(q)(|\alpha\cap q|)
. That is,
\alpha^{\prime}
is the element of
red_{\gamma}(q)
which corresponds to
\alpha
via the increasing bijection from
red_{\gamma}(q)
to
q
. Since
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
, there is
F\in\mathcal{F}
so that
red_{\gamma}(q)\subseteq F_{0}
and
F\cap\alpha^{\prime}=R(F).
Let
p=Cut_{\alpha_{q}+1}(F)
. We claim that
p
is an element of
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha,A}
which is smaller that
q
. Indeed, since
\alpha_{p}+1\in red_{\gamma}(q)
, then
(\alpha_{q}+1)^{-}_{k_{q}}=q\cap\gamma
. Furthermore,
\rho^{F}>k_{q}
because
F_{0}
contains the reduction of
q
to
\gamma
. In this way,
p\leqslant q
by virtue of the Lemma
5.1.10
. Moreover, by virtue of the Remarks
5.1.7
and
5.1.8
, the following chain of inclusions hold:

A\subseteq q=Cut_{\alpha_{q}+1}(red_{\gamma}(q))\subseteq Cut_{\alpha_{q}+1}(F_{0})=p_{0}.

Now, let
\phi:F\longrightarrow Cut_{\alpha_{q}+1}(F)
be the increasing bijection. Note that
red_{\gamma}(q)\backslash\alpha_{q}
is an initial segment of
F
. According to the last part of the Remark
5.1.7
,
\phi[red_{\gamma}(q)]=Cut_{\alpha_{q}+1}(red_{\gamma}(q))=q
. In particular,
\phi[\alpha^{\prime}]=\alpha.
Since
\phi[R(F)]=R(p)
, we have that
R(p)=\phi[\alpha^{\prime}\cap F]=\alpha\cap p
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.20
.

Suppose that
\tau
is a good type and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a filter over
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
intersecting
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{D}_{F}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha,A}
for all
\alpha\in\omega+\gamma
,
A\in\text{FIN}(\omega+\gamma)
and
F\in\mathcal{F}
1
1

1

Such
\mathcal{G}
exists due to the Rasiowa-Sikorski Lemma
. Then
\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{G}}
is a construction scheme over
\gamma+\omega
which contains
\mathcal{F}
and satisfies
IH_{1}
.

Proof.

If we use that
\mathcal{G}
intersects each
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{\alpha}
, and argue in a similar fashion as we did in Lemma
5.0.13
, it is easy to see that
\mathcal{F}^{G}
is a construction scheme over
\gamma+\omega
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{G}}
because
\mathcal{G}
intersects each
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{F}}
, and
\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{G}}
satisfies
IH_{1}
because
\mathcal{G}
intersects each
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha,A}.

∎

Suppose that
\tau
is a good type, By means of the results
5.1.12
,
5.1.20
,
5.0.13
, and
5.1.13
, we can recursively construct, for each limit
\gamma<\omega_{1}
, a construction scheme
\mathcal{F}^{\gamma}
which satisfies
IH_{1}
and such that
\mathcal{F}^{\delta}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{\gamma}
whenever
\delta<\gamma
. If we use one more time the Lemmas
5.0.13
and
5.1.13
, we can conclude that

\mathcal{F}=\bigcup\limits_{\gamma\in Lim}\mathcal{F}^{\gamma}

is a construction scheme over
\omega_{1}
which satisfies
IH_{1}
. Thus, we have proved Theorem
2.2.5
.

Theorem
2.2.5
.
For any good type there is a construction scheme over
\omega_{1}
of that type which satisfies
IH_{1}.

5.2
Diamond principle and FCA(part)

The purpose of this section is to prove that Jensen’s
\Diamond
-principle implies
FCA
. In order to do this, we will work in the same manner as we did in the Subsection
5.1.1
. That is, we want to find a suitable property
IH_{2}
which is satisfied by
\mathcal{F}(\omega)
. Furthermore, given a construction scheme
\mathcal{F}
over a limit ordinal
\gamma\leqslant\omega_{1}
, we want the two following things to happen:

(a)

If
\gamma
is a limit of limit ordinals and
\mathcal{F}|_{\delta}
satisfies
IH_{2}
for each limit
\delta<\gamma
, then
\mathcal{F}
also satisfies
IH_{2}
.

(b)

If
\gamma
is countable and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
then there is a construction scheme
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
over
\gamma+\omega
containing
\mathcal{F}
which satisfies
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
.

By doing this, we may conclude that there is a construction scheme over
\omega_{1}
which satisfies
IH_{2}
. Finally, we want that:

(c)

Any construction scheme over
\omega_{1}
which satisfies
IH_{2}
is fully capturing.

While the points (a) and (c) are relatively easy to guarantee, it turns out that finding a property
IH_{2}
which also satisfies the point (b) is a non-trivial problem.

For the rest of this section, we fix a countable limit ordinal
\gamma
and a construction scheme
\mathcal{F}
be over
\gamma
of type
\tau.
Moreover, we fix a
\Diamond
-sequence
\langle D_{\alpha}\rangle_{\alpha\in\text{Lim}}
.

Definition 5.2.1

(Block interval sequence)
.

Let
j\in\omega
and
A
be a non-empty subset of
\omega_{1}
. We say that a sequence
\mathbb{I}=\langle\mathbb{I}(i)\rangle_{i<j}\subseteq\text{FIN}(A)
is a
block interval sequence over
A
if:

\blacksquare

For any
i<t
,
\mathbb{I}(i)
is an interval in
A
.

\blacksquare

For all
i<i^{\prime}<t
,
\mathbb{I}(i)<\mathbb{I}(i^{\prime})
.

Given
\alpha\in\omega_{1}
, we let
Bl(\alpha,A)
to be the set of all interval sequences over
A\backslash\alpha.
2
2

2

Note that
Bl(\alpha,A)=Bl(0,A\backslash\alpha)
for all
\alpha\in\omega_{1}
and
A\in\text{FIN}(\omega_{1}).
Given
\alpha\in X
, we denote
Bl(\,\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k},\,m_{k})
simply as
Bl_{k}(\alpha)
for each
k\in\omega
.

Remark 5.2.2
.

Note that a block interval sequence
\mathbb{I}
may be empty even though its elements are not. This case is not pathological, and will be highly relevant for the proof of Theorem
5.2.16
.

Definition 5.2.3
.

Suppose that
A
and
B
are non-empty subsets of
\omega_{1}
and
\phi:A\longrightarrow B
is an increasing function. Given
\mathbb{I}=\langle\mathbb{I}(i)\rangle_{i<j}\in Bl(0,A)
, we let

\phi\circ\mathbb{I}=\langle\,\phi[\mathbb{I}(i)]\,\rangle_{i<j}.

Recall that if
B
has size
m
for some
m\in\omega
, then we identity the increasing bijection
\phi:m\longrightarrow B
with
B
, and we denote the inverse of such function as
B^{-1}
. Following this convention, if
A=m
and
\mathbb{I}
is as before,
\phi\circ\mathbb{I}
is denoted as
B\circ\mathbb{I}.
Analogously, if
\mathbb{J}\in Bl(0,B)
, we denote
\phi^{-1}\circ\mathbb{J}
as
B^{-1}\circ\mathbb{J}
.

Remark 5.2.4
.

Suppose that
A,B,\phi
and
\mathbb{I}
are as in the previous definition. Since
\phi
is increasing, then
\phi\circ\mathbb{I}(i)<\phi\circ\mathbb{I}(i^{\prime})
for all
i<i<j
. However, it is not necessarily true that
\phi\circ\mathbb{I}(i)
is an interval for a given
i.
A particular case in which this occurs is when
im(\phi)
is itself an interval in
B
. In fact, it is easy to show that
im(\phi)
is an interval in
B
if and only if

\{\phi\circ\mathbb{I}\,:\,\mathbb{I}\in Bl(0,A)\}\subseteq Bl(0,B).

Moreover, if
\phi
is surjective then the equality between the previous sets will hold. Particularly, if
\alpha\in\gamma
and
k\in\omega
, then

Bl(\alpha,F)=\{\,F\circ\mathbb{I}\,:\,\mathbb{I}\in Bl_{k}(\alpha)\,\},

Bl_{k}(\alpha)=\{F^{-1}\circ\mathbb{I}\,:\,\mathbb{I}\in Bl(\alpha,F)\,\}

for all
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
with
\alpha\in F.

In here,
F
represents an element of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
with
\alpha\in F
and
\mathbb{I}
is an element of
Bl(\alpha,F)
.

Definition 5.2.5

(Good pairs and good sets)
.

Let
\alpha\in\omega_{1}
and
A\in\text{FIN}(\omega_{1})
. we say that a pair
(\mathbb{I},z)
is
(\alpha,A)
-good
if the following conditions hold:

\blacksquare

z\in A\backslash\alpha,

\blacksquare

\mathbb{I}\in Bl(\alpha,A\cap(z+1))
. That is,
\mathbb{I}\in Bl(\alpha,A)
and either
\mathbb{I}=\emptyset
or
\max(\bigcup\mathbb{I})\leqslant z.

A non-empty set of ordered pairs
T
is said to be
(\alpha,A)
-Good
if every
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
is
(\alpha,A)
-good. We denote the family of
(\alpha,A)
-Good sets as
Good(\alpha,A)
. Given
\alpha\in\gamma
and
k\in\omega
, we denote
Good(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k},m_{k})
as
Good_{k}(\alpha)
.

In here,
(\mathbb{I},z)
represents an
(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k},m_{k})
-good pair.

Definition 5.2.6
.

Suppose that
A
and
B
are non-empty subsets of
\omega_{1}
and
\phi:A\longrightarrow B
is an increasing function. Given
\alpha\in\omega_{1}
and
T\in Good(\alpha,A)
we define

\phi\bullet T=\{\,(\phi\circ\mathbb{I},\phi(z))\,:\,(\mathbb{I},z)\in T\,\}.

In the particular case where
|B|=m
for some
m\in\omega
and
A=m
, we denote
\phi\bullet T
as
B\bullet T
. Analogously, if
T^{\prime}\in Good(\alpha,B)
then
\phi^{-1}\bullet T^{\prime}
is denoted as
B^{-1}\bullet T^{\prime}.

Remark 5.2.7
.

If
\alpha\in\gamma
and
k\in\omega
then

Good(\alpha,F)=\{F\bullet T\,:\,T\in Good_{k}(\alpha)\,\},

Good_{k}(\alpha)=\{F^{-1}\bullet T\,:\,T\in Good(\alpha,F)\,\}

for each
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}
with
\alpha\in F.

For the next the definition, it is convenient to recall the function
\phi^{\beta}_{l}
that was presented in Definition
5.0.22
.

Definition 5.2.8
.

Let
\beta\in\gamma
and
k\leqslant l\in\omega
. We define
\pi^{k,l}_{\beta}:\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k}+1\longrightarrow\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}+1
as:

\pi^{k,l}_{\beta}=\phi^{\beta}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\beta}_{k})^{-1}.

It is not hard to see that
\pi_{\alpha}^{k,l}
is just the increasing bijection from
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k}+1
to
(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l})_{k}.

Definition 5.2.9

(The
\bigstar
relation)
.

Let
\beta,\xi\in\gamma
,
2\leqslant k<l\in\omega
, and
T\in Good_{k}(\beta).
We say that
T
guesses
(\beta,\xi,k,l)
, and write it as
T\bigstar(\beta,\xi,k,l)
, if there is
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
for which:

(a)

\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}
and
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}=z
,

(b)

(\xi)_{k}\circ\mathbb{I}\in Bl(0,(\xi)_{l}).
Equivalently, if
\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}\circ\mathbb{I}\in Bl(0,m_{l}),

(c)

(\xi)_{l}(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l})\in(\xi)_{k}.
Equivalently,
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\lVert_{l})_{k}.

In here, the pair
(\mathbb{I},z)
represent an element of
T
which testifies that
T\star(\beta,\xi,k,l).

Remark 5.2.10
.

If
T\bigstar(\beta,\xi,k,l)
and
\xi^{\prime}
is such that
\lVert\xi^{\prime}\rVert_{l}=\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}
then
T\bigstar(\beta,\xi^{\prime},k,l).

Remark 5.2.11
.

The previous definition will often be applied in cases where
\xi<\beta
.

Recall that we fixed
\langle D_{\delta}\rangle_{\delta\in\text{Lim}}
a
\Diamond
-sequence at the start of this section.

Definition 5.2.12

(The
\checkmark
relation)
.

Let
\beta\in\gamma
,
k\leqslant l\in\omega
and
\delta\in Lim
. Given
T\in Good_{k}(\beta)
and
C\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
, we say that
(C,\delta)
accepts
(T,\beta,k,l)
, and write it as
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)
, if the following conditions hold:

(1)

C\subseteq D_{\delta}
and
C
is captured at level
l
,

(2)

T\bigstar(\beta,\,C(0),\,k,\,l-1),
.

(3)

|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}
. Equivalently, if
F\in\mathcal{F}_{l}
is such that
C\subseteq F
, then
(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta=R(F).

Whenever there is
C
for which
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)
, such
C
has cardinality at least
1
and at most
n_{l}
due to the point (1). Thus, we can define
j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)
as the maximum of such cardinalities. That is:

j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)=\max(\,j\leqslant n_{l}\,:\,\exists C\in[\gamma]^{j}\,(\,(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)\,)\,).

If there is no such
C
, then we define
j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)
as
0
.

Definition 5.2.13

(The
IH_{2}
property)
.

Given
\delta\in\text{Lim}\cap\gamma
, we say that
IH_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds if one of the two following mutually excluding conditions occurs:

(A)

There are infinitely many
l\in\omega
for which there is
C\in\text{FIN}(D_{\delta})
which is fully captured at level
l
. In this case, we will say that
IH^{A}_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds.

(B)

For all
\beta\in[\delta,\gamma)
,
2\leqslant k\in\omega
and
T\in Good_{k}(\beta)
there are infinitely many
k\leqslant l\in\omega
for which
j=j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)<n_{l}
and such that:

(B.1)

|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l},

(B.2)

\Xi_{\beta}(l)=j,

(B.3)

If
j>0
then there is
C\in[\delta]^{j}
for which
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)
and
C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}.

In this case we will say that
IH^{B}_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds.

Finally, we say that
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{2}
if
IH_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds for any
\delta\in\text{Lim}\cap\gamma
.

The two following results follow directly from the definition.

Proposition 5.2.14
.

\mathcal{F}(\omega)
satisfies
IH_{2}
.

Lemma 5.2.15
.

Assume that
\gamma
is a limit of limit ordinals and for each limit
\gamma^{\prime}<\gamma
,
\mathcal{F}|_{\gamma^{\prime}}
satisfies
IH_{2}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{2}
.

More important, the property
IH_{2}
in fact implies full capturing when
\gamma=\omega_{1}
.

Theorem 5.2.16
.

Suppose that
\gamma=\omega_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{2}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is fully capturing.

Proof.

We will prove the Theorem by appealing to the equivalence of fully capturing stated in Lemma
2.4.15
. Let
S\in[\omega_{1}]^{\omega_{1}}
. Since
\langle D_{\delta}\rangle_{\delta\in\text{Lim}}
is a
\Diamond
-sequence, there is
\delta\in\text{Lim}
so that:

1.

S\cap\delta=\delta
.

2.

(\delta,<,D_{\delta},\mathcal{F}|_{\delta})
is an elementary submodel of
(\omega_{1},<,S,\mathcal{F}).

Suppose towards a contradiction that there is no
C\in\text{FIN}(S)
so that
C
is fully captured. By elementary, the same is true for
D_{\delta}
. In other words,
IH_{2}^{A}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
fails and consequently
IH_{2}^{B}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds. Let us fix
\beta\in S\backslash\delta
and
k=2.
We now define
\mathbb{I}=\emptyset
,
z=\lVert\beta\rVert_{2}
and
T=\{(\mathbb{I},z)\}\in Good_{2}(\beta)
. According to the point (B) in Definition
5.2.13
, there is
2\leqslant l\in\omega
for which
j=j(\delta,T,\beta,2,l)<n_{l}
and such that:

\blacksquare

|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}
,

\blacksquare

\Xi_{\beta}(l)=j
,

\blacksquare

If
j>0
then there is
C\in[\delta]^{j}
for which
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,2,l)
and
C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}
.

We will arrive to the desired contradiction by finding a finite set
C^{\prime}\in\text{FIN}(\omega_{1})
for which
(C^{\prime},\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,2,l)
and
|C^{\prime}|=j+1
. The proof of this is divided into two cases:

Case 1
: If
j=0.

Proof of case.

In this case, as
\Xi_{\beta}(l)=0
, it is straightforward that
\{\beta\}
is captured at level
l
. By elementarity, there is
\xi\in D_{\delta}
so that
\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}=\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}
. In particular,
\{\xi\}
is captured at level
l
. The proof of this case ends by proving the following claim.

Claim 1
:
(\{\xi\},\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)
.

Proof of claim.

It is enough to show that
T\bigstar(\beta,\xi,2,l-1)
. For this, note that the point
(a)
of Definition
5.2.9
is satisfied for the unique pair
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
because
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}=\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}
. In particular,
\Delta(\beta,\xi)>l
so
\lVert\xi\rVert_{2}=\lVert\beta\lVert_{2}=z
. The point
(b)
of Definition
5.2.9
holds because
\mathbb{I}=\emptyset
. Therefore,
\pi^{2,l}_{\xi}=\emptyset\in Bl(0,m_{l})
. Finally, the point
(c)
of Definition
5.2.9
is satisfied because
(\xi)_{l}(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l})=(\xi)_{l}(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})=\xi
.
∎

∎

Case 2
: If
j>0
.

Proof of case.

Let
C\in[\delta]^{j}
be such that
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,2,l)
and
C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}
. By elementarity, there is
\xi\in D_{\delta}
so that
\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}=\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}
and
C\subseteq(\xi)_{l}
. Particularly,
\Xi_{\xi}(l)=j
and
\rho(C(i),\xi)\leqslant l
for each
i<j
. In fact, as
\Xi_{C(i)}(l)=i
because
C
is captured at level
l
, we have that
\rho(C(i),\xi)=l
due to Lemma
2.3.9
.
We will finish the proof of this case by showing the next claim.

Claim 2
:
(C\cup\{\xi\},\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)
.

Proof of claim.

It suffices to prove that
C\cup\{\xi\}
is captured at level
l.
For this, we appeal to the Proposition
2.4.10
. By the previous observations, we only need to prove that
\Delta(C(i),\xi)=\rho(C(i),\xi)
for each
i<j
. First note that, according to the point
(2)
in Definition
5.2.12
,
T\bigstar(\beta,C(0),2,l-1)
. As
(\emptyset,\lVert\beta\rVert_{2})
is the only element of
T
, then
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\leqslant\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}
,
\lVert C(0)\rVert_{2}=\lVert\beta\rVert_{2}
and
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\in(\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1})_{2}
. In other words,
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}
is in the domain of
\phi^{\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}}_{2}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\phi^{\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}}_{2}(\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1})=\lVert\,\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}\,\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert C(0)\rVert_{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert\beta\rVert_{2}=\lVert\,\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\,\rVert_{2}=\phi^{\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}}_{2}(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1})

due to the part
(a)
of Proposition
5.0.23
. Since
\phi^{\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l}}_{2}
is one to one, it follows that
\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}=\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}
. Now, as
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}=\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}
and
\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}=\lVert C(i)\rVert_{l-1}
, then
\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1}=\lVert C(i)\rVert_{l-1}
. That is,
l\leqslant\Delta(C(i),\xi)\leqslant\rho(C(i),\xi)\leqslant l
. This finishes the proof.
∎

∎

∎

Before starting the next subsection, we present the key concept needed for proving Theorem
5.2.26
.

Definition 5.2.17

(Transferring Good sets)
.

Let
\beta\in\gamma
,
k\in\omega
and
T\in Good_{k}(\beta)
. Given
k^{\prime}>k
, we define
\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}}(\beta,T)
as the set of all pairs
(F,\mathbb{I})\in\mathcal{F}_{k}(m_{k^{\prime}})\times Bl_{k}(\beta)
such that:

\blacksquare

There is
z\in m_{k}
such that
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
,

\blacksquare

\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\in F
,

\blacksquare

F\circ\mathbb{I}\in Bl_{k^{\prime}}(\beta).
In other words,
F[\mathbb{I}(i)]
is an interval in
m_{k}^{\prime}
for every
i<\mathbb{I}.

Given
\alpha\in(\beta)^{-}_{k^{\prime}}(
and
(F,\mathbb{I})\in\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}}(\beta,T)
, let
\mathbb{I}_{F}=(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\beta,\rVert_{k^{\prime}}]^{\smallfrown}(F\circ\mathbb{I})
3
3

3

In principle,
\mathbb{I}_{F}
also depends on
\alpha
. However, we will never use this concept with two different
\alpha
’s at the same time. For that reason, we omit any mention of that ordinal in the notation.
. That is,
\mathbb{I}_{F}=\langle\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)\rangle_{i<\mathbb{I}+1}
is given by:

\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)=\begin{cases}(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}]&\textit{ if }i=0\\
F[\mathbb{I}(i-1)]&\textit{ if }i>0\end{cases}

We define
the transferring of
T
relative to
k^{\prime},\alpha
and
\beta
as:

\text{Transf}_{k^{\prime}}(T,\alpha,\beta)=\{\,(\mathbb{I}_{F},\,F(z))\,:\,(\mathbb{I},z)\in T\textit{ and }(F,\mathbb{I})\in\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}}(\beta,T)\,\}

In the first picture, we considered a a pair
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
and
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}(m_{k^{\prime}})
with
\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\in F
. In the second picture, we verified that
F\circ\mathbb{I}
is an element of
Bl_{k^{\prime}}(\beta)
. As this is the case, we can conclude that
(\mathbb{I}_{F},F(z))\in\text{Transf}_{k^{\prime}}(T,\alpha,\beta)
.

Remark 5.2.18
.

Note that if
T\in Good_{k}(\beta)
,
\alpha\in(\beta)^{-}_{k}
and
k^{\prime}>k
then
Transf_{k^{\prime}}(T,\alpha,\beta)\in Good_{k^{\prime}}(\alpha).

Lemma 5.2.19
.

Let
\beta\in\gamma
,
k\in\omega
,
T\in Good_{k}(\beta)
,
k^{\prime}>k
and
\alpha\in(\beta)^{-}_{k^{\prime}}
. Suppose that
l>k^{\prime}
and
\xi\in\gamma
are such that:

(1)

\lVert\beta\lVert_{l}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{l},

(2)

(\xi)_{l}(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l})\in(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}.
In other words,
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k^{\prime}}

(3)

(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}[\,(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}]\,]=(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}\backslash(\alpha+1)
is an interval in
(\beta)_{l}.

Then the following statements are equivalent:

(a)

T\bigstar(\beta,\xi,k,l).

(b)

\text{Transf}_{k^{\prime}}(T,\alpha,\beta)\bigstar(\alpha,\xi,k^{\prime},l).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}}(\beta,T)
and
T^{\prime}=\text{Transf}_{k^{\prime}}(T,\alpha,\beta)
.

(a)\Rightarrow(b)
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
so that
\lVert\beta\rVert_{k}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}=z
,
\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}\circ\mathbb{I}\in Bl(0,m_{l})
and
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k}
. Now, let us take
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}(m_{k^{\prime}})
having
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}
as an element. Before we continue, we will highlight some useful facts:

\blacksquare

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\in F
so
F\cap(\xi+1)=(\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}})_{k}
. Consequently,
F(a)=(\phi_{k}^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}})^{-1}(a)
for each
a\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}
.

\blacksquare

\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}=\phi^{(\lVert{\xi}\rVert_{k^{\prime}})}_{k}\circ\phi^{\xi}_{k}(\xi)
according to Lemma
5.0.24
. Therefore,
F(\lVert\xi\rVert_{k})=\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}
by virtue of the previous point.

\blacksquare

Again, by Lemma
5.0.24
:

\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}\circ\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}=\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}\circ\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\xi}_{k})^{-1}=\phi^{\xi}_{k^{\prime}}\circ(\phi^{\xi}_{k})^{-1}=(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}}_{k})^{-1}.

Claim 1
:
(F,\mathbb{I})\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Proof of claim.

First we will show that
\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\in F
. Indeed, since
k\leqslant k^{\prime}<l
and
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k}
then, by Proposition
5.0.23
and Lemma
5.0.24
, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}=\lVert\,\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\,\rVert_{k^{\prime}}=\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l})

\displaystyle\in\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}[\,(\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}\,)_{k}\,]

\displaystyle=(\,\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})\,)_{k}

\displaystyle=(\,\lVert\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}\,\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\,)_{k}=(\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}})_{k}\subseteq F.

Now, let
i<|\mathbb{I}|
. The claim will be over once we prove that
F[\mathbb{I}(i)]
is an interval in
m_{k^{\prime}}
. As
\mathbb{I}(i)\leqslant z=\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}
then
F[\mathbb{I}(i)]=(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}}_{k})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}(i)]=\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}\circ\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}(i)].
By virtue of the hypotheses,
\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}]
is an interval in
m_{l}
which is contained in
(\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}\,)_{k}
. Hence, such interval is also included in
(\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}\,)_{k^{\prime}}
. Since,
\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}
is the increasing bijection from
(\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}\,)_{k^{\prime}}
to
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}
, it follows that
\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}[\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}]]
is an interval in
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}+1
. From this it is straightforward that
F[\mathbb{I}(i)]
is an interval in
m_{k^{\prime}}
. This finishes the claim.
∎

Since
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
and
(F,\mathbb{I})\in\mathcal{Q}
then
(\mathbb{I}_{F},F(z))\in T^{\prime}
. We will show that such pair testifies that
T^{\prime}\bigstar(\alpha,\xi,k^{\prime},l)
. This will be achieved by proving the three following claims.

Claim 2
:
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l}\leqslant\rVert\xi\rVert_{l}
and
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}=F(z).

Proof of claim.

We know that
z=\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}
, and it has already been proved that
F(\lVert\xi\rVert_{k})=\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}
. As
\alpha\in(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}
then
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l}\leqslant\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}
. But we know by the hypotheses that
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}
. So we are done

∎

Claim 3
:
\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}\circ\mathbb{I}_{F}\in Bl(0,m_{l}).

Proof of claim.

Let
i<|\mathbb{I}_{F}|
. We want to show that
\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]
is an interval in
m_{l}
. First we deal with the case where
i=0
. Here,
\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)=(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}]
. We will first argue that, in this case,
\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]=\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\beta}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]
. Indeed, since
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k^{\prime}}
then
\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}|_{\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}+1}=\phi^{\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}}.
In particular, this means that
(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[a]=(\phi^{\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[a]
for each
a\leqslant\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}
. In this way, by using Lemma
5.0.24
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\xi}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ((\phi^{\xi}_{l})^{-1}\circ(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1})[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]

\displaystyle=(\phi^{\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]=(\phi^{\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]

\displaystyle=(\phi^{\beta}_{l}\circ((\phi^{\beta}_{l})^{-1})\circ(\phi^{\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\beta}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\beta}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]=\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\beta}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]

Observe that
(\phi^{\beta}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}(i)]=(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}[\,(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}]\,]
is an interval in
(\beta)_{l}
, according to the point (3) in the hypotheses of this lemma. Since
\phi^{\beta}_{l}
is the increasing bijection from
(\beta)_{l}
to
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}+1\sqsubseteq m_{l}
, then
\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\beta}[\mathbb{I}(i)]
is an interval in
m_{l}
. This finishes the first case. Now we deal with the case where
i>0
. Here,
\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)=F[\mathbb{I}(i-1)]
. Thus,

\displaystyle\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\xi}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[F(i)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\xi}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}[(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}}_{k})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}(i-1)]]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ((\phi^{\xi}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}\circ(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}}_{k})^{-1})[\mathbb{I}(i-1)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\xi}_{k})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}(i-1)]=\pi_{\xi}^{k,l}[\mathbb{I}(i-1)]

Due to the hypotheses,
\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}(i-1)]
is already an interval in
m_{l}
. Consequently, this case is over.
∎

Claim 4
:
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k^{\prime}}.

Proof of claim.

Recall that
\alpha\in(\beta)_{k}
. Therefore,
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l})_{k}
due to the point (b) of Proposition
5.0.23
. As
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k^{\prime}}
, we are done.
∎

∎

(b)\Rightarrow(a)
.

Let
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
and
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}(m_{k^{\prime}})
with
(F,\mathbb{I})\in\mathcal{Q}
be such that
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}=F(z)
,
\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}\circ\mathbb{I}_{F}\in Bl(0,m_{l})
and
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k^{\prime}}
. We will show in the next three claims that
(\mathbb{I},z)
testifies that
T\bigstar(\beta,\xi,k^{\prime},l)
.

Claim 5
:
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}
and
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}=z.

Proof of claim.

Due to the hypotheses of the lemma, we already know that
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}
. Therefore it suffices to prove that
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}=z
. Indeed, as
F\in\mathcal{F}_{k}(m_{k^{\prime}})
then
\lVert F(z)\rVert_{k}=z
. Moreover, we know that
F(z)=\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}
. Thus,
z=\lVert F(z)\rVert_{k}=\lVert\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\,\rVert_{k}=\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}
by virtue of the point (a) in Proposition
5.0.23
.

∎

Claim 6
:
\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}\circ\mathbb{I}\in Bl(0,m_{l}).

Proof of claim.

Let
i<|\mathbb{I}|
. We need to prove that
\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}(i)]
is an interval in
m_{l}
. First note that
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\in F
, so as in the proof of the other direction of the lemma,
F\cap(\xi+1)=(\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}})_{k}
. In this way,
F(a)=(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}}_{k})^{-1}(a)
for each
a\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}.
From this fact, we get the following chain of equalities:

\displaystyle\pi^{k,l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}(i)]=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ(\phi^{\xi}_{k})^{-1}[\mathbb{I}(i)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ((\phi^{\xi}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}\circ(\phi^{\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}}_{k})^{-1})[\mathbb{I}(i)]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\xi}_{l}\circ\phi^{\xi}_{k^{\prime}}[F[\mathbb{I}(i)]]

\displaystyle=\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(i+1)]

As
\pi^{k^{\prime},l}_{\xi}\circ\mathbb{I}\in Bl(0,m_{l})
, we are done.
∎

Claim 7
:
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})_{k}
.

Proof of claim.

On one hand,
\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}\in F
since
(F,\mathbb{I})\in\mathcal{Q}
. On the other hand, we already know that
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k}\in F
. Therefore,
\rho(\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}})\leqslant k
. Now, according to the hypotheses
\rho(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l},\lVert\beta\rVert_{l})\leqslant{k^{\prime}}
. Thus,

\rho(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l},\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})=\rho(\lVert\,\lVert\beta\rVert_{l}\,\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\,\lVert\xi\rVert_{l}\,\rVert_{k^{\prime}})=\rho(\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lvert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}})

by virtue of the points (a) and (b) of Proposition
5.0.23
. In this way,
\rho(\lVert\beta\rVert_{l},\lVert\xi\rVert_{l})\leqslant k
.

∎

∎

∎

Corollary 5.2.20
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a construction scheme over an ordinal
\gamma
. Also, let
\beta\in\gamma
,
T\in Good_{k}(\beta)
,
k^{\prime}>k
and
\alpha\in(\beta)^{-}_{k^{\prime}}
. Suppose that
(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}\backslash(\alpha+1)
is an interval in
(\beta)_{l-1}
. Furthermore, assume that
l>k^{\prime}
and
\delta\in\text{Lim}\cap\alpha
are such that
|(\alpha)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}
. If
C\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
is such that
(C,\delta)\checkmark(k,l,\beta,\delta,T)
then
(C,\delta)\checkmark(k^{\prime},l,\beta,\delta,T^{\prime})
where
T^{\prime}=\text{Transf}_{k^{\prime}}(\alpha,\beta,T)
. In particular,
j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)\leqslant j(\delta,T^{\prime},\alpha,k^{\prime},l)
.

Proof.

Let
C\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
be such that
(C,\delta)\checkmark(k,l,\beta,\delta,T^{\prime})
. Since
\alpha\in(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}
and
l>k^{\prime}
then
(\beta)_{l}\cap(\alpha+1)=(\alpha)_{l}
. By the hypotheses,
\alpha\geqslant\delta
. This means that
(C(0))_{l}[r_{l}]=(\beta)_{l}\cap\delta=(\alpha)_{l}\cap\delta
. The only thing left to prove is that
T^{\prime}\bigstar(\alpha,C(0),k^{\prime},l-1)
. For this, it suffices to show that the hypotheses of Lemma
5.2.19
hold for
\xi=C(0)
. Indeed, since
T\bigstar(\beta,\xi,k,l-1)
then
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1})_{k}
. This implies both that
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1}
and
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1})_{k^{\prime}}
. According to the assumptions
(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}[\,(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\,\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}]\,]
is an interval in
(\beta)_{l-1}
. Thus, we are done.

∎

5.2.1
Forcing the property
IH_{2}

In this subsection we aim to prove that if
\gamma
is a countable ordinal and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
, then we can extend
\mathcal{F}
to a construction scheme over
\gamma+\omega
which also satisfies
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
. For this, we will make use of the forcing
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. In Section
5.1
we already proved that there is a countable family of dense sets over
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
so that if
G
is a filter intersecting each of those sets, then
\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{G}}
is construction scheme over
\gamma+\omega
which also satisfies
IH_{1}
. Here we want to show the same result but for the Property
IH_{2}
. However, due to the large amount of variables appearing in the Definition
5.2.13
, the explicit formula for each of those dense sets is quite messy. For that reason, we will instead show that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
forces
\mathcal{F}^{Gen}
to satisfy
IH_{2}
. Before doing this, it is worth pointing out some last remarks regarding the forcing
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F}).

Remark 5.2.21
.

By virtue of the the Lemmas
5.1.17
,and
5.1.19
, we have that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\mathcal{F}^{Gen}$ is construction scheme over $\gamma+\omega$ which satisfies $IH_{1}$\textquotedblright}.

Remark 5.2.22
.

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{Gen}$\textquotedblright}
due to the Lemma
5.1.18
. This means that
\rho_{F^{Gen}}|_{\gamma^{2}}
is forced to be equal to
\rho_{\mathcal{F}}
. Therefore, there is no risk of confusion by referring to both of these two ordinal metrics simply as
\rho
.

Remark 5.2.23
.

If
p\in\mathbb{P}_{k}(\mathcal{F})
for some
k\in\omega
, then
p\vDash\text{\textquotedblleft $p\in\mathcal{F}^{Gen}_{k}$ \textquotedblright}.
In particular,
\mathcal{F}(p)
is forced to be equal to
\mathcal{F}^{Gen}|_{p}
. In other words,
\rho|_{p^{2}}=\rho_{\mathcal{F}(p)}
. So, again, there is no risk of confusion on referring to
\rho_{\mathcal{F}(p)}
, simply as
\rho
.

Remark 5.2.24
.

Suppose that
p\in\mathbb{P}_{l}(\mathcal{F})
,
\beta\in p
and
k\leqslant l
. Then for each
H\in\mathcal{F}_{j}(p)
it happens that
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $H\cap(\beta+1)=(\beta)_{k}$ \textquotedblright}.
4
4

4

Here, by
(\beta)_{k}
we mean
k
-closure of
\beta
calculated inside
\gamma+\omega
with respect to
\rho_{\mathcal{F}^{Gen}}
.
That is,
p
decides the value of
(\beta)_{k}
. Furthermore, this value can be calculated using
\mathcal{F}(p)
. From this, it follows that
p
also knows who is
\lVert\beta\rVert_{k}
,
\Xi_{\beta}(k)
,
Bl_{k}(\beta)
(
=Bl(\lVert\beta\rVert_{k},m_{k})
) and
Good_{k}(\beta)
. Therefore, there should not be any confusion while working explicitly with this sets instead of with their names, of course, as long as the conditions at the beginning of this remark hold.

Lemma 5.2.25
.

Let
q\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. Then there is
p\leqslant q
so that
|p\cap\gamma|=r_{k_{p}+1}
.

Proof.

From Lemma
5.1.17
, it is easy to get
q^{\prime}\leqslant q
so that
k_{q^{\prime}}>k_{q}
. Now, according to Lemma
5.1.19
, there is
p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
so that
p^{\prime}\leqslant q^{\prime}
and
p^{\prime}\cap\gamma=R(p^{\prime})
. Put
p=p^{\prime}_{0}
. Then
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
and
p\leqslant q
. Furthermore,
|p\cap\gamma|=|R(p^{\prime})|=r_{k_{p^{\prime}}}=r_{k_{p}+1}.

∎

Theorem 5.2.26
.

Suppose that
\gamma
is countable and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
. Then

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\mathcal{F}^{Gen}$ satisfies $IH_{2}$ \textquotedblright}.

Proof.

According to the Definition
5.2.13
, our goal is to prove that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $IH_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F}^{Gen})$ holds \textquotedblright}

for any
\delta\in\text{Lim}\cap(\gamma+\omega)
. If
\mathcal{F}
satisfies that there are infinitely many
l\in\omega
for which there is
C\in\text{FIN}(D_{\delta})
which is fully captured at level
l
, it is easy to see that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\vDash\text{\textquotedblleft $IH^{A}_{2}(\gamma,\mathcal{F}^{Gen})$ holds \textquotedblright}
. This is because
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\mathcal{F}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{Gen}$ \textquotedblright}
. Therefore, the only interesting case happens when:

With respect to
\mathcal{F}
, there is
k^{\prime\prime}\in\omega
such that for each
l\geqslant k^{\prime\prime}
there is no
C\in\text{FIN}(D_{\gamma})
which is fully captured at level
l
.

From now on, let us assume that this case holds.

Claim
:
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $IH^{B}_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F}^{Gen})$ holds \textquotedblright}.

Proof of claim.

Let
q\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
,
\beta\in[\delta,\gamma+\omega)
,
2\leqslant k\in\omega
and a name
\dot{T}
for an element of
Good_{k}(\beta)
. Because of Lemma
5.1.17
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\beta\in q
and
q\in\mathbb{P}_{k^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})
for some
k^{\prime}>k,k^{\prime\prime}
. According to the Remark
5.2.24
,
Good_{k}(\beta)
is fully determined by
q
. In particular, this means that there is
T
such that
q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $T=\dot{T}$ \textquotedblright}
.

We need to show that there is
l>{k^{\prime}}
and
p\leqslant q
with the following properties:

\blacksquare

p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}$ \textquotedblright}.

\blacksquare

There is
j\in\omega
so that
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $j=j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)$ \textquotedblright}
.

\blacksquare

p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\Xi_{\beta}(l)=j$ \textquotedblright}
.

\blacksquare

If
j>0
then there is
C\in[\gamma+\omega]^{j}
so that
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)$ and $C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}$\textquotedblright}
.

We will divide the rest of the proof into two cases.

Case 1
:
\delta=\gamma
.

Proof of case.

By virtue of the Lemma
5.2.25
, there is
q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
so that
q^{\prime}\leqslant q
and
|q^{\prime}\cap\gamma|=r_{k_{q}+1}
. Let
l=k_{q}+1
. Note that the objects needed to calculate
j(\gamma,T,\beta,k,l)
are
\mathcal{F}^{Gen}|_{\gamma}
,
T
,
(\beta)_{l-1}
,
k
an
l
. All of this objects are already decided by
q^{\prime}
. Therefore, there is
j\in\omega
so that
q^{\prime}\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $j=j(\gamma,T,\beta,k,l)$\textquotedblright}
.

Subcase 1.1
: If
j=0
.

Proof of subcase.

Consider
F\in\mathcal{F}_{l}
so that
q^{\prime}\cap\gamma\sqsubseteq F
. Then
R(F)=q^{\prime}\cap\gamma
. Let
\alpha=\min(F_{0}\backslash R(F))
and
p=Cut_{\alpha}(F)
. It is straightforward that
R(p)=q^{\prime}\cap\gamma
and
q^{\prime}=p_{0}
. This means that
p\leqslant q
. Moreover, as
\beta\in q\backslash\gamma
then
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft$|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}$ \textquotedblright}
and
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\Xi_{\beta}(l)=0=j$ \textquotedblright}
. In this case, there is nothing more to prove.
∎

Subcase 1.2
: If
j>0
.

Proof of subcase.

By definition of
j
, there is
C\in[\gamma+\omega]^{j}
so that
(C,\gamma)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)
. According the point (1) of Definition
5.2.12
,
C\subseteq D_{\gamma}
and
C
is captured at level
l
. Let
F\in\mathcal{F}_{l}
be so that
C\subseteq F
. Then, by the point (3) of Definition
5.2.12
, it follows that
R(F)=(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\gamma=q^{\prime}\cap\gamma
. In this case, let
\alpha=\min(F_{j}\backslash R(F))
and
p=Cut_{\alpha}(F)
. As in the previous case, we have
p_{j}=q^{\prime}
and
R(p)=q^{\prime}\cap\gamma
. Thus,
q^{\prime}\leqslant p_{j}
. Again, as
\beta\in q\backslash\gamma
then
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}$ \textquotedblright}
and
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\Xi_{\beta}(l)=j$ \textquotedblright}
. In order to finish, note that
p\cap\gamma=\bigcup\limits_{i<j}F_{i}
. In particular,
C\subseteq p\cap\gamma\subseteq(\beta+1)\cap p=(\beta)_{l}
. That is,

p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $(C,\gamma)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)$ and $C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}$ \textquotedblright}.

∎

∎

Case 2
: If
\delta<\gamma
.

Proof of case.

If
\beta<\gamma
there is nothing to do. This is because
\mathcal{F}
is forced to be contained in
\mathcal{F}^{Gen}
and
\mathcal{F}
already satisfies
IH_{2}
. Therefore, we may assume that
\beta\geqslant\gamma
. Appealing to Lemma
5.1.17
, we may assume without loss of generality that
q\cap[\delta,\gamma)\not=\emptyset
. Let
\alpha=\alpha_{q}=\max(q\cap\gamma)\geqslant\delta
. The plan is apply the property
IH_{2}
to
\alpha
(inside
\mathcal{F}
). For this purpose, let
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}}(\beta,T)
and
T^{\prime}=\text{Transf}_{k^{\prime}}(T,\alpha,\beta)
.

By our assumptions
IH^{A}_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
does not hold. Therefore,
IH^{B}_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds. This means that there is
l\geqslant k^{\prime}
for which
j^{\prime}=j(\delta,T^{\prime},\alpha,k^{\prime},l)<n_{l}
and such that:

(I)

|(\alpha)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l},

(II)

\Xi_{\alpha}(l)=j,

(III)

If
j>0
then there is
C\in[\gamma]^{j}
for which
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T^{\prime},\alpha,k^{\prime},l)
and
C\subseteq(\alpha)_{l}
.

Before we continue, we remark that:

\blacksquare

q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\alpha\in\beta\cap q=(\beta)^{-}_{k^{\prime}}$ \textquotedblright}
.

\blacksquare

q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}\backslash(\alpha+1)=[\gamma,\beta+1)$ \textquotedblright}
. Since
[\gamma,\beta+1)
is already an interval in
\gamma+\omega
, it follows that
q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $(\beta)_{k^{\prime}}$ is an interval in $(\beta)_{l-1}$\textquotedblright}
.

\blacksquare

q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $(\beta)^{-}_{l-1}\cap\alpha=(\alpha)_{l-1}$ \textquotedblright}
because
l>k^{\prime}
. In particular, this means that
q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=|(\alpha)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}$ \textquotedblright}.

\blacksquare

Since
j^{\prime}\geqslant 0
and
\Xi_{\alpha}(l)=j^{\prime}
then
q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\Xi_{\beta}(l)=j^{\prime}$ \textquotedblright}
by means of the Lemma
2.3.9
.

Due to the first two points, the hypotheses of Corollary
5.2.20
are fulfilled. In particular, this means that

q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft$j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)\leqslant j^{\prime}$ \textquotedblright}.

We will divide the rest of the proof into two subcases.

Subcase 2.1
: If
j^{\prime}=0
.

Proof of subclaim.

As
j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)
is always non-negative then

q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft$j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)=0$ \textquotedblright}.

Because of this and by the last two points of the previous remark,
p=q
forces everything that we want.
∎

Subcase 2.2
: If
j^{\prime}>0
.

Proof of subcase.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}_{l}
be such that
\alpha\in F
. Since
j>0
, there is
C\in[\gamma]^{j}
for which
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T^{\prime},\alpha,k^{\prime},l)
and
C\subseteq(\alpha)_{l}
. Note that
q\vDash\text{\textquotedblleft $(\alpha)_{l}\subseteq(\beta)_{l}$\textquotedblright}
. In this way,
q\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}$ \textquotedblright}
. Our next task will be to extend
q
to a condition forcing that
T\bigstar(\beta,C(0),k,l-1)
. By appealing to Lemma
5.2.19
, it suffices to find
p\leqslant q
which forces that
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\leqslant\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}
and
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\in(\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1})_{k^{\prime}}
.

As
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T^{\prime},\alpha,k^{\prime},l)
then
T^{\prime}\bigstar(\alpha,C(0),k^{\prime},l-1)
. Let
\xi\in F_{j}
be such that
\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1}=\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}
. By Remark
5.2.10
,
T^{\prime}\bigstar(\alpha,\xi,k^{\prime},l-1)
. Thus, there are
(\mathbb{I},z)\in T
and
(G,\mathcal{I})\in\mathcal{Q}
so that
(\mathbb{I}_{G},G(z))
testifies the previous guessing relation. That is:

(a)

\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l-1}\leqslant\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1}
and
\lVert\xi\rVert_{k^{\prime}}=G(z)
. Since both
\xi
and
\alpha
are in
F_{j}
then
\rho(\alpha,\xi)\leqslant l-1
. Thus, this point implies that
\alpha\leqslant\xi
.

(b)

(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}\circ\mathbb{I}_{G}\in Bl(0,(\xi)_{l-1}).

(c)

(\xi)_{l-1}(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l-1})\in(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}.
As
\alpha,\xi\in F_{j}
then
(\xi)_{l-1}(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l-1})=F_{j}(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{l-1})=\alpha
. Therefore, this point implies that
\alpha\in(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}
.

According to the point (b),
(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}[\mathbb{I}_{G}(0)]=(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}[\,(\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}},\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}]]
is an interval in
(\xi)_{l-1}
. Moreover, by the point (a), this interval is contained in
F_{j}\backslash R(F)
. Therefore, it is also an interval in
F
. Let
\alpha^{\prime}=(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}[\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}}+1]
. In other words,
\alpha^{\prime}
is the successor of
\alpha
inside
(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}
. Because of this, it follows that
(\alpha^{\prime})^{-}_{k^{\prime}}=(\alpha)_{k^{\prime}}=q\cap\gamma
. In this way,
p=Cut_{\alpha^{\prime}}(F)\leqslant q
due to the Lemma
5.1.10
.

Subclaim 1
:
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\leqslant\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}$ and $\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\in(\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1})_{k^{\prime}}$ \textquotedblright}
.

Proof of subclaim.

Let
\phi:F\longrightarrow p
be the increasing bijection. By the definition of the Cut function, it follows that
\phi(\alpha^{\prime})=\gamma
. Now, since
(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}[\mathbb{I}_{F}(0)]
is an interval in
F
, then
\phi[\,(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}[\mathbb{I}_{G}(0)]\,]=(\phi(\xi))_{k^{\prime}}[\mathbb{I}_{G}(0)]
is an interval in
p
. Furthermore, the first point of this interval is
\gamma
. In this way, as
p\backslash\gamma
is an initial segment of
[\gamma,\gamma+\omega)
, we conclude that
\phi((\xi)_{k}^{\prime}(\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}))
(the last point of the interval
\phi[(\xi)_{k^{\prime}}[\,\mathbb{I}_{G}(0)]\,]
) is equal to

\gamma+(\lVert\beta\rVert_{k^{\prime}}-\lVert\alpha\rVert_{k^{\prime}})=\beta.

As direct consequence of this equality, p forces that
\beta\leqslant\phi(\xi)
and
\rho(\beta,\phi(\xi))\leqslant{k^{\prime}}
. It then follows that
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\leqslant\lVert\phi(\xi)\rVert_{l-1}
and
\lVert\beta\rVert_{l-1}\in(\lVert\phi(\xi)\rVert_{l-1})_{k^{\prime}}
. Since
\lVert\phi(\xi)\rVert_{l-1}=\lVert\xi\rVert_{l-1}=\lVert C(0)\rVert_{l-1}
, we are done.
∎

By virtue of the previous subclaim,
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $T\bigstar(\beta,C(0),k,l-1)$ \textquotedblright}
. It then follows that
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $(C,\delta)\checkmark(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)$ \textquotedblright}
. As
|C|=j^{\prime}
, we get that

p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $j^{\prime}\leqslant j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)$ \textquotedblright}.

Hence, these two numbers are equal. In conclusion, we have shown that there are
p\leqslant q
and
j\in\omega
(namely,
j^{\prime}
), so that:

\blacksquare

p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l}$ \textquotedblright}
and
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $\Xi_{\beta}(l)=j$ \textquotedblright}
.
6
6

6

This was already forced by
q
.

\blacksquare

There is
C\in[\gamma+\omega]^{j}
so that
p\Vdash\text{\textquotedblleft $(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)$ and $C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}$\textquotedblright}
.

Thus, the proof of this subcase is over.
∎

∎

∎

∎

It is not hard to see that the property forced in the previous theorem can be coded by countable many dense subsets of
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
. In this way, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 5.2.27
.

Suppose that
\gamma
is a limit ordinal and
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
. There is a countable family of dense sets in
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{F})
so that if
\mathcal{G}
is a filter intersecting all of them, then
\mathcal{F}^{\mathcal{G}}
is a construction scheme over
\gamma+\omega
which contain
\mathcal{F}
and satisfies both
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
.

Now we have proved all the necessary results to show that there is a fully capturing construction scheme over
\omega_{1}
of type
\tau
. Namely, Propositions
5.1.12
and
5.2.14
, Lemmas
5.1.13
and
5.2.15
, and Corollary
5.2.27
. From them we conclude that there is a sequence
\langle\mathcal{F}^{\delta}\rangle_{\delta\in\text{Lim}}
so that for all
\delta<\gamma\in\text{Lim}
, the following properties hold:

\blacksquare

\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
is a construction scheme over
\delta
satisfying
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
,

\blacksquare

\mathcal{F}_{\delta}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\gamma}
.

It follows that
\mathcal{F}=\bigcup\limits_{\delta\in\text{Lim}}\mathcal{F}^{\delta}
is a fully capturing construction scheme. This is due to the Theorem
5.2.16
.

Theorem 5.2.28
.

The
\Diamond
-principle implies
FCA
.

Now fix
\mathcal{P}
an aribtrary partition of
\omega
compatible with
\tau
. Consider the following variations of the the properties
IH_{1}
and
IH_{2}
.

Definition 5.2.29

(The
IH_{1}(\mathcal{P})
property)
.

We say that
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{1}(\mathcal{P})
if for all
A\in\text{FIN}(\gamma)
,
P\in\mathcal{P}
and
\alpha\in\gamma
, there is
F\in\mathcal{F}
with
\rho^{F}\in P
such that
IH_{1}(\alpha,A,F)
holds.

Definition 5.2.30

(The
IH_{2}(\mathcal{P})
property)
.

Given
\delta\in\text{Lim}\cap\gamma
and
P\in\mathcal{P}
, we say that
IH_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds if one of the two following mutually excluding conditions occurs:

(A)

There are infinitely many
l\in P
for which there is
C\in\text{FIN}(D_{\delta})
which is fully captured at level
l
. In this case, we will say that
IH^{A}_{2}(P,\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds.

(B)

For all
\beta\in[\delta,\gamma)
,
2\leqslant k\in\omega
and
T\in Good_{k}(\beta)
there are infinitely many
k\leqslant l\in P
for which
j=j(\delta,T,\beta,k,l)<n_{l}
and such that:

(B.1)

|(\beta)_{l-1}\cap\delta|=r_{l},

(B.2)

\Xi_{\beta}(l)=j,

(B.3)

If
j>0
then there is
C\in[\delta]^{j}
for which
(C,\delta)\checkmark(T,\beta,k,l)
and
C\subseteq(\beta)_{l}.

In this case we will say that
IH^{B}_{2}(P,\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds.

Finally, we say that
\mathcal{F}
satisfies
IH_{2}(\mathcal{P})
if
IH_{2}(\delta,\mathcal{F})
holds for any
\delta\in\text{Lim}\cap\gamma
and each
P\in\mathcal{P}
.

All the results stated in this section are true when
IH_{1}
is changed by
IH_{1}(\mathcal{P})
and
IH_{2}
is changed by
IH_{2}(\mathcal{P})
. Moreover, the proofs are completely similar. In this way, we deduce theorem
2.4.19
.

Theorem 5.2.31
.

The
\Diamond
-principle implies
\text{FCA}(part)
.

5.3
P-ideal Dichotomy and capturing axioms

Throughout this work, we have seen a handful of applications of construction schemes. Many of the objects that we have constructed using capturing construction schemes already contradict to some degree the
P
-ideal dichotomy. For example, since the Suslin Hypothesis follows from
PID
(see
[
4
]
and
[
123
]
), this axiom is incompatible with
FCA
. In addition, since
PID
implies that any gap is indestructible,
CA_{3}
also contradicts
PID
. However, note that none of the applications that we have of
CA_{2}
is enough to contradict the
P
-ideal dichotomy. For now, the best we have is that
PID+\min(\mathfrak{b},\,cof(\mathcal{F}_{\sigma})\,)>\omega_{1}
is incompatible with the existence of a
2
-capturing construction scheme. This is because such assertion is equivalent to the nonexistence of a sixth Tukey type (see
[
85
]
). The purpose of this section is to prove that
PID
is in fact incompatible with
CA_{2}
.

For the remainder of this section, we will assume that
PID
holds and that
\mathcal{F}
is a construction scheme. Our plan is to define a
P
-ideal
\mathcal{I}
from
\mathcal{F}
that contradicts
PID
whenever
\mathcal{F}
is
2
-capturing. This ideal
\mathcal{I}
will be defined as family of countable sets of the orthogonal ideal of some other ideal
\mathcal{H}
. When applying the second alternative of
PID
to the ideal
\mathcal{I}
, we will need to calculate who is
\mathcal{I}^{\perp}
. In general, this may be a difficult task. However, the following concept will facilitate these calculations.

Definition 5.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an ideal of countable sets in
\omega_{1}
. We will say that
\mathcal{H}
is
Frechet in
[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}
if for any
A\in\mathcal{H}^{+}
, there is an infinite
B\subseteq A
such that
B\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
.

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an ideal of countable sets in
\omega_{1}
and assume that
\mathcal{H}
is Frechet in
[\omega_{1}]^{\omega_{1}}
. Then
(\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\leqslant\omega})^{\perp}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}=\mathcal{H}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}
.

Proof.

We will only show that the inclusion from right to left holds. For this purpose, let
A\in(\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\leqslant\omega})^{\perp}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}
. Assume towards a contradiciton that
A\not\in\mathcal{H}
. Then
A\in\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}
. Since
\mathcal{H}
is Frechet, there is an infinite
B\subseteq A
such that
B\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}
. In particular,
A\cap B
is infinite, and this contradicts the fact that
A\in(\mathcal{H}^{\perp})^{\perp}
. Thus, the proof is over.

∎

We now define the ideal
\mathcal{I}
which will be used in the proof of Theorem
5.3.11
.

Definition 5.3.3
.

Given
n\in\omega
and
\alpha\in\omega_{1}
, we define

H_{n}(\alpha)=\{\xi<\alpha\,:\,\forall m>n\,(\,\Xi_{\alpha}(m)=-1\textit{ or }\Xi_{\xi}(m)\leqslant\Xi_{\alpha}(m)\,)\}.

Additionally, we define the ideal generated by the family
\{H_{n}(\alpha)\,:\,n\in\omega\textit{ and }\alpha\in\omega_{1}\}
as
\mathcal{H}
. Lastly, we define
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{H}^{\perp}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\leqslant\omega}.

Remark 5.3.4
.

[\omega_{1}]^{<\omega}
is included in both
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{I}.

The following lemma is easy.

Lemma 5.3.5
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma<\omega_{1}
and
m,n\in\omega
. Then:

(1)

If
m<n
, then
H_{m}(\alpha)\subseteq H_{n}(\alpha).

(2)

If
\beta<\gamma
and
n>\rho(\beta,\gamma)
, then
H_{n}(\gamma)\cap\beta\subseteq H_{n}(\beta).

Proof.

The point (1) follows directly from the definition. In order to prove (2) we will show that
\omega_{1}\backslash H_{n}(\beta)\subseteq\omega_{1}\backslash(H_{n}(\gamma)\cap\beta)
. Let
\xi\in\omega_{1}\backslash H_{n}(\beta)
. If
\xi\geqslant\beta
we are done, so let us assume that
\xi<\beta.
Then there is
m>n
for which
\Xi_{\xi}(m)>\Xi_{\beta}(m)\geqslant 0
. Note that
m>\rho(\beta,\gamma)
. Thus, by the point (c) of Lemma
2.3.9
we conclude that
\Xi_{\beta}(m)=\Xi_{\gamma}(m)
. In this way,
\xi\not\in H_{n}(\gamma)
. This finishes the proof.

∎

Remark 5.3.6
.

By the point (1) of the previous lemma we have that for any
A\in\mathcal{H}
there are
n\in\omega
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\omega_{1}
for which
A\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{i<n}H_{n}(\alpha_{i}).

Definition 5.3.7
.

Given
\beta\in\omega_{1}
we define
\mathcal{H}|_{\beta}
as the ideal generated by the family
\{H_{n}(\alpha)\,:\,\alpha\leqslant\beta\textit{ and }n\in\omega\}.

Lemma 5.3.8
.

Let
\beta<\omega_{1}
and
A\subseteq\beta
.

(a)

If
A\in(\mathcal{H}|_{\beta})^{\perp}
, then
A\in\mathcal{H}^{\perp}.
Furthermore, since
A
is countable then
A\in\mathcal{I}.

(b)

If
A\in(\mathcal{H}|_{\beta})^{+}
, then
A\in\mathcal{H}^{+}.

Proof.

Proof of
(a)
.

It is enough to show that
A\cap H_{n}(\gamma)=^{*}\emptyset
for any
\beta\leqslant\gamma\in\omega_{1}
and each
n\in\omega
. Furthermore, we may assume that
n>\rho(\beta,\gamma)
due to the point (1) of Lemma
5.3.5
. Indeed, by virtue of the point (2) of the same lemma, we have that

A\cap H_{n}(\gamma)=A\cap\beta\cap H_{n}(\gamma)\subseteq A\cap H_{n}(\beta)=^{*}\emptyset.

∎

Proof of
(b)
.

Suppose towards a contradiction that
A\notin\mathcal{H}^{+}
. Then there are
n\in\omega
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\omega_{1}
for which
A\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{i<n}H_{n}(\alpha_{i}).
Without any loss of generality we may assume that there is
j<n
so that, for any
i<n
,
\alpha_{i}\leqslant\beta
if and only if
i<j
. Furthermore, we may assume that
n>\rho(\beta,\alpha_{i})
for any
i\geqslant j
. From this, it follows that

A\subseteq\big(\bigcup\limits_{i<j}H_{n}(\alpha_{i})\big)\cup\big(\bigcup\limits_{j\leqslant i<n}H_{n}(\alpha_{i})\cap\beta\big)\subseteq\big(\bigcup\limits_{i<j}H_{n}(\alpha_{i})\big)\cup\big(\bigcup\limits_{j\leqslant i<n}H_{n}(\beta)\big).

In this way,
A\in\mathcal{H}|_{\beta}
which is a contradiction.

∎

∎

Proposition 5.3.9
.

\mathcal{H}
is Frechet in
[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}.

Proof.

Let
A\in\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}
and
\beta\in\omega_{1}
be such that
A\subseteq\beta
. Note that
A\in(\mathcal{H}|_{\beta})^{+}
. Since
\mathcal{H}|_{\beta}
is a countably generated ideal, there is
B\in[A]^{\omega}
such that
B\in(\mathcal{H}|_{\beta})^{\perp}
. According to the point
(a)
of Lemma
5.3.8
,
A\in\mathcal{I}
. Thus, the proof is over.

∎

Proposition 5.3.10
.

\mathcal{I}
is a
P
-ideal.

Proof.

Let
\langle A_{n}\rangle_{n\in\omega}
be an increasing sequence of elements of
\mathcal{I}
. Then there is
\beta\in\omega_{1}
for which
\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}A_{n}\subseteq\beta
. Let us enumerate
\beta+1
as
\langle\beta_{n}\rangle_{n\in\omega}
. We then define

A=\bigcup\limits_{n\in\omega}\big(A_{n}\backslash\big(\bigcup\limits_{i\leqslant n}H_{n}(\beta_{i})\big)\big).

By the Lemma
5.3.5
, it follows that
A
is a pseudo-union of
\langle A_{n}\rangle_{n\in\omega}
and
A\in(\mathcal{H}|_{\beta})^{\perp}
. In particular, this means that
A\in\mathcal{I}
due to the point (a) of Lemma
5.3.8

∎

Theorem 5.3.11
.

\mathcal{F}
is not
2
-capturing.

Proof.

Suppose towards a contradiction that
\mathcal{F}
is
2
-capturing. According to the Proposition
5.3.10
,
\mathcal{I}
is a
P
-ideal. Since we are assuming
PID
, there are two possibilities for
\mathcal{I}
. We will finish the proof by showing that both of them lead to a contradiciton.

Case 1
: There is an uncountable
Y\subseteq\omega_{1}
such that
[Y]^{\omega}\subseteq\mathcal{I}.

Non satisfaction of case.

Consider the coloring
c:[Y]^{2}\longrightarrow 2
given by:

c(\{\alpha,\beta\})=0\text{ if and only if }\{\alpha,\beta\}\text{ is captured}.

Using the partition relation
\omega_{1}\longrightarrow(\omega_{1},\omega+1)^{2}_{2}
and the fact that
\mathcal{F}
is
2
-capturing, we can conclude that there is an increasing sequence
\langle\alpha_{i}\rangle_{i<\omega+1}\subseteq Y
such that
\{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\}
for any two distinct
i,j<\omega+1
. In particular,
\{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{\omega}\}
is captured for any
i<\omega
. By lemma
2.3.9
, we get that
A=\{\alpha_{i}\,:\,i<\omega\}\subseteq H_{0}(\alpha_{\omega})
. In other words,
A\in\mathcal{I}
. This is a contradiction, so this case can not occur.

∎

Case 2
: There
Y
an uncountable subset of
\omega_{1}
such that
[Y]^{\omega}\subseteq\mathcal{I}^{\perp}.

Non satisfaction of case.

According to Proposition
5.3.9
,
\mathcal{I}
is Frechet over
[\omega_{1}]^{\omega}
. From this it follows that
[Y]^{\omega}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
. Let

P=\{l\in\omega\,:\,\exists\alpha\in Y\,(\,\Xi_{\alpha}(l)>0\,)\}.

By refining
Y
, we may assume that for any
l\in P
there are uncountable many
\alpha\in Y
for which
\Xi_{\alpha}(l)>0
. In this way, we can recursively construct sequences
\langle A_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta\in\omega_{1}}\subseteq[Y]^{\omega}
,
\langle B_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta\in\omega_{1}}\subseteq\text{FIN}(\omega_{1})
and
\langle n_{\beta}\rangle_{\beta\in\omega_{1}}
so that the following properties hold for any two
\beta<\gamma\in\omega_{1}
:

(1)

A_{\beta}<B_{\beta}<A_{\gamma}
. In particular
B_{\beta}\cap B_{\gamma}=\emptyset.

(2)

For all
l\in P
there is
\alpha\in A_{\beta}
for which
\Xi_{\alpha}(l)>0
.

(3)

A_{\beta}\subseteq\bigcup\limits_{\alpha\in B_{\beta}}H_{n_{\beta}}(\alpha).

(4)

B_{\beta}\cap Y\not=\emptyset.

By refining such sequence, we can suppose that there is
n\in\omega
such that
n_{\beta}=n
for any
\beta\in\omega_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{F}
is
2
-capturing, there are
\beta<\gamma\in\omega_{1}
such that
\{B_{\beta},B_{\gamma}\}
is captured at some level
l>n
. Now,
B_{\beta}
and
B_{\gamma}
are disjoint. Thus,
\Xi_{\alpha}(l)=1
for any
\alpha\in B_{\gamma}
. Therefore,
l\in P
due to the point (4). By virtue of the point (2), there must be
\xi\in A_{\beta}
such that
\Xi_{\xi}(l)>0
. On the other hand,
\Xi_{\alpha}(l)=0
for all
\alpha\in B_{\beta}.
Consequently,
\xi\not\in H_{n}(\alpha)
for any such
\alpha
. This contradicts the point (3), so this case can not occur.

∎

∎

Corollary 5.3.12
.

(Under
PID
) There are no
2
-capturing construction schemes.

Chapter 6
Open problems

6.1
Problems about gaps

One of the most important concepts introduced in Section
3.1
was the Luzin representation of a partial order and what it means for a Luzin family to code a partial order (see Definition
3.1.15
) Indeed, in order to prove that there is a Luzin-Jones family
\mathcal{A}
in
ZFC
, we ended up proving that such family
\mathcal{A}
we can differentiatet is natural to ask if the same is true for any Luzin-Jones family.

Problem 6.1.1
.

Is there a Luzin-Jones family which doesn’t code
\omega_{1}
?

A partial answer to this question is “no”. If
\mathfrak{b}>\omega_{1}
then any Luzin family is Jones and it codes
\omega_{1}
. Thus, a related problem would be whether the existence of a Luzin-Jones family which doesn’t code
\omega_{1}
follows from the equality
\mathfrak{b}=\omega_{1}
. Although such a family might exist, it is still possible that there is a partial order
X
which characterizes the Jones property in terms of coding: Formally:

Problem 6.1.2
.

Is there a partial order
X
so that for any Luzin family, coding
X
and being Jones are equivalent statements?

Given an almost disjoint family
\mathcal{A}
, let us define the
spectrum of
\mathcal{A}
, namely
Spec(\mathcal{A})
, as the class of all partial orders
(X,<)
such that
\mathcal{A}
codes
X
(Definition
3.1.15
). The class
Spec(\mathcal{A})
can be regarded as a measurement of the “malleability” of
\mathcal{A}
. From the point of view of the author, it is an interesting problem to know how much we can differentiate an almost disjoint family from another one by analysing their spectrums.

Problem 6.1.3
.

Which are the possible values of
Spec(\mathcal{A})
for a given Luzin family
\mathcal{A}
?

Let
\mathcal{X}_{\omega_{1}}
denote the class of
\omega_{1}
-like orders. In Theorem
3.1.20
, we have seen that the Luzin-Jones family
\mathcal{A}
that we constructed codes each element of
\mathcal{X}_{\omega_{1}}
. Following with Problem
6.1.1
, we may ask how strong is the property of coding each
\omega_{1}
-like order in relation to the property of being Luzin-Jones. We may ask also ask how much of the spectrum do we need to know in order to assure that it contains
\mathcal{X}_{\omega_{1}}
. That is:

Problem 6.1.4
.

Is there a finite family
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\omega_{1}}
so that, for any Luzin-Jones family
\mathcal{A}
, if
\mathcal{Y}\subseteq Spec(\mathcal{A})
, then
\mathcal{X}_{\omega_{1}}\subseteq Spec(\mathcal{A})
?

Now we move to problems about gaps in a more general context. It is a theorem of Rothberger that
\mathfrak{b}
is the minimal cardinal
\kappa
for which
(\omega,\kappa)
-gaps exist. In fact, when we restrict to cardinals, the only gaps that we can construct in
ZFC
alone are the ones of type
(\omega_{1},\omega_{1})
,
(\omega,\mathfrak{b})
and
(\mathfrak{b},\omega)
. Indeed, if we assume
PFA
then these are the only posibilities (see
[
113
]
). However, as we have shown in Theorem
3.1.21
, the situation becomes much more complex when we consider
(X,Y)
-gaps where
X
and
Y
have cofinality
\omega_{1}
. We wonder if the same level of complexity carries to
(X,Y)
-gaps when
|X|=\omega
and the cofinality of
Y
is
\mathfrak{b}
. Before stating our problem, let us consider the natural generalisation of orders similar to
\omega_{1}
.

Definition 6.1.5
.

Let
\kappa
be a cardinal and
(X,<)
be a partial order. Let us call
(X,<)
a
\kappa
-like order
whenever
X
is well-founded,
|X|=\kappa
, and
|(-\infty,x)|<\kappa
for each
x\in X
.

We then ask:

Problem 6.1.6
.

Is there is an
(\omega,X)
-gap for any
\mathfrak{b}
-like order
X
? Does
PFA
decides this question?
